chapter 7Semantic SegmentationTechniques, Challenges, and Applications in Intelligent Transportation Systems

Nouman Latif, Muhammad Saadi, and Demostenes Zegarra Rodriguez

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472506-7

7.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence is helping human beings in almost every field. For example, if we are using a mobile phone for taking pictures, artificial intelligence (AI) is used for refining it. You can see the AI working when using social media. You search about some topic and advertisements start appearing on your social media account about it. AI helps in detecting and preventing diseases. AI is playing a role in protecting the environment. It helps bankers, businessmen, and almost everyone in analysis to make different decisions. AI is starting to become an integral part of cars, aircraft, gadgets, medical devices, machines, etc. There are numerous applications of AI and it is progressing day by day. The field of communication is one of the many, which is being revolutionized by the use of AI. Communication is used in many applications, and the whole world is connected at different levels through communication systems. The Internet was revolutionary. It connects people through different devices. But now, with the progress in AI, not only humans are communicating but also machines are also communicating with each other. The communication between machines is called the Internet of things (IoT). This technology allows different sensors and machines to share data. A variant of IoT is the Internet of vehicles (IoV). In IoV, different vehicles communicate with each other on roads and share information with each other which can be crucial for safety. This field has come into existence because of the progressing technology of autonomous vehicles. An autonomous driving system (ADS) requires information from different types of sensors and from other vehicles to make a decision. One of the most important and computationally extensive data is image which has a vital role in environment perception. Images are to be transmitted from sensor to vehicle controller in intra-vehicle communication, from one vehicle to other vehicles in vehicle-to-vehicle (V2V) communications, and from vehicle-to-infrastructure in vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I) communication. Apart from intelligent transportation systems, transmitting images in any system is challenging. AI is also used to increase the efficiency of this task whether it is wired communication or wireless communication. The techniques used for this application utilize different deep learning approaches that deduct semantic information from the image, discard redundant information, and transmit only the semantic information through a communication channel. This chapter will discuss semantic segmentation, its types, techniques, and its implementation in communication systems for efficient and low-latency transmission of images.

7.2 Image Segmentation

Any system of AI needs information using which it can make decisions and perform any task such as predicting the price of a house, generating images, writing stories, determining whether a patient has a disease or not, guiding an autonomous vehicle, and predicting a cyber-attack. For the tasks in which information is required for an AI system to see the world like humans do, image segmentation is used. It is a fundamental technique in machine vision that is used to extract information from images or videos. The extracted information should be useful and it is possible if we divide an image into some segments or parts. In image segmentation, this division is performed on the pixels that belong to a certain group based on similarity between the pixels. For example, consider the image in Figure 7.1 which contains different objects or classes:

If we want an AI system to define this image, then it can do it efficiently if the information of the image is divided into parts such as grass, ropes, human, sheep, and a dog. Image segmentation will make groups of pixels in such a way that pixels belonging to a human will make one segment, pixels for a dog will make another segment, there will be a segment of sheep, and then for the grass. Using this useful coherent information about the segments in an image, now an AI system or a machine vision system can interpret this image. The image segmentation divides the image into meaningful parts by measuring the similarity of the pixels in terms of certain characteristics such as textures, intensity, or color. Figure 7.2 shows a segmented image in which different objects shown in the image of Figure 7.1 are labeled using different colors such as background pixels are labeled as green, pixels related to humans are sky blue, sheep are labeled with dark blue color, and the dog is represented using pixels of red color.

7.2.1 Types of Image Segmentation

Grouping the pixels in an image based on similarities between them is segmentation and depending on the methodology and scope of segmentation provides us with different types of segmentation. The following are the types of image segmentation:

7.2.1.1 Thresholding-Based Segmentation

An image consists of pixels, and each pixel has a value between 0 and 255. If it is a grayscale image, then there will be only one channel, and if it is a color image, then there will be three channels. In thresholding, if there is a substantial difference between the pixel values of one object and the background, a threshold value is decided. Pixels with values lower than the threshold will be grouped into one segment and pixels with values greater than the threshold will be assigned another segment. This technique is also called binarization of an image as there will be only two segments in the output. For example, all background pixels are converted into black pixels, and all pixels related to the region of interest are converted into black color pixels. This can be observed in Figure 7.3. In this figure, the region of interest is turned white and the background is converted as black pixels.

7.2.1.2 Edge-Based Segmentation

To differentiate different objects in an image, edge detection can be used to identify the boundaries. It works on the principle that when we pass through an edge in an image, an abrupt change is observed. This change can tell us if there is a boundary or edge at that instance. For example, if we want to differentiate a car from its background, then we can use edge detection. Consider Figure 7.4 to understand this concept. In Figure 7.4a, different types of beans are shown and we can observe boundaries between these types. Using edge-based detection, these boundaries are clearly differentiating types of beans in an image as shown in Figure 7.4b.

7.2.1.3 Cluster-Based Segmentation

In machine learning, clustering is a type of unsupervised learning in which the data has no labels, and based on the similarity between different data points, the clusters are made. For segmentation, this approach can be used for differentiating different objects in an image. This method is useful because the pixels in an image are also unlabeled. Different techniques are available for cluster-based segmentation. One of these methods is K-means clustering in which K-number clusters are made based on different values of centers equal to the number of clusters. For example, in Figure 7.5, the value for K is 2 which means the image is segmented into two clusters. One for the background and one for the class animals.

7.2.1.4 Graph-Based Image Segmentation

Segmentation is all about coherence between the pixels based on which different parts in an image are grouped into segments. One of the emerging and fast-growing techniques in machine learning is graph neural networks (GNU). This method is used for various types of data but we can also use it for image segmentation. For this purpose, an image is converted into a graphical representation with pixels as nodes and connectivity showing coherence between the pixels by using edges. Figure 7.6 shows the segmentation of an image using graphs. The graph of the original image is divided into subgraphs, and based on the similarity between the pixels, different clusters are made as shown in Figure 7.6b.

7.2.1.5 Semantic Segmentation

The difference between the different types discussed previously and semantic segmentation is that labels are given to different objects in an image based on the similarity between the pixels. Moreover, the methodology used for this purpose is supervised in nature, such as a very famous technique called fully convolutional networks (FCN) in which labeled images are used to train a network to get the semantic information. Consider an example in Figure 7.7. In the original image, we can observe that there are riders on bikes and then there is a road and an audience. The semantic segmentation is shown in Figure 7.7b; it can be observed that pixels for bikes, humans, and backgrounds are grouped as different segments.

7.2.1.6 Instance-Based Segmentation

This type of segmentation has taken semantic segmentation one step ahead as it even differentiates or labels the pixels of the same class. To understand this concept, first consider Figure 7.7 in which humans are grouped in one cluster, background in one, and audience in another cluster. Instance segmentation will not only group the pixels based on similarity but also it can group clusters within clusters. It will also label the humans such as Mike riding a bike on the left and Jhon riding the bike on the right. Figure 7.8 shows an example of instance segmentation.

In Figure 7.8a, there are three humans and then there is a background with different objects. If we use instance segmentation to differentiate between the humans and the background, it will also group the humans in different clusters as shown by different colors in Figure 7.8b. If this was semantic segmentation, all humans would have been colored the same. So, instance segmentation gives more granular semantic information.

7.2.1.7 Deep Learning-Based Segmentation

In all of the previous types, we have discussed that different objects in an image are segmented based on their similarity. This similarity is between the texture or color or edges which represent the features of the objects. Usually, these features are first extracted using hand-crafted techniques, but in deep learning, an end-to-end pipeline works to automatically extract these features and then perform the segmentation. The deep learning approach is convolutional neural network (CNN) based. Figure 7.9 shows how the quality of segmentation is improved by adapting the deep learning approach.

7.3 Intelligent Transportation System (ITS)

With an increasing population, the need for transport is also increasing. With this expansion in ground transportation, there is a need for enhanced safety, security, efficiency, and reliability. To handle different aspects of this system, ITS utilizes different technologies, including information, control, and communications. ITS is a vast field that covers various aspects of transport systems such as:

  • Traffic management
  • Traffic surveillance and monitoring
  • Intelligent parking management
  • Vehicle-to-vehicle communication
  • Vehicle-to-infrastructure communication
  • Autonomous driving system (ADS)
  • Intelligent toll collection system
  • Incident/accident detection system
  • Smart emergency response system

This list is not inclusive, but many other aspects are included in ITS. If we observe the above-mentioned elements of ITS, we can say that image classification is used in almost every task such as to detect the movement of vehicles; video is used for traffic management, surveillance, and monitoring; images of vehicles or registration plates can be used; for a parking management system, images of empty spaces can be used.

Also, it can be noted that in all these elements, we can see that there is a need for communication between different nodes which includes the transmission and reception of the images or video.

7.3.1 Importance of Semantic Segmentation in Communication Systems for ITS

As mentioned earlier, in the intelligent transportation system (ITS), data transmission is a vital block that is responsible for transmitting and receiving different types of data such as images, text, video, or audio. But mostly, visual information is required to be exchanged. Moreover, this data exchange should be fast enough because safety-critical decisions are to be made based on this information. There are two types of communication in ITS, one is wired and the other one is wireless. As an example of wired communication, we can discuss an autonomous vehicle in which there is a huge amount of data that is to be transferred between sensors, controllers, and actuators. This is called intra-vehicle communication which includes interaction between human/vehicle and between vehicle and a thing. This communication should be fast, robust, and accurate as the safety of the vehicle is dependent on it. The example of wireless communication involves the exchange of data between different vehicles on the road which provide information to each other for increasing the decision capabilities of the ADS of all the autonomous cars on the road. This communication between the vehicles or from a vehicle to an infrastructure is also called IoV or inter-vehicle communication. Figure 7.10 shows the different modes of communication such as intra-vehicle, inter-vehicle (V2V), and vehicle-to-infrastructure (V2I).

Regardless of which type of communication model is used, the data communication system should have lower latency and high throughput and should utilize less bandwidth. In order to understand the importance of semantics in communication, first understand the meaning of semantics, which is context or meaning. As a human being, if we see an image, we not only know about all the objects in an image but also understand what is happening in an image. To understand this concept, consider Figure 7.11.

Without context, we can see that this image has a certain number of people; there are objects such as a table, a laptop, and a notice board. The understanding without a context or meaning is not sufficient to grasp the complete information in the image. So, if we see the image with context, we will come to the conclusion that there is a meeting going on in the image, and probably a deal has been made or two groups have come to an understanding, which is why they are shaking hands and everyone looks happy.

The traditional communication system does not care for the context in an image, it just transmits the information of pixels in the form of 1’s and 0’s. It transmits the raw data in the form of bits.

But on the way to the receiver, in the channel, if even a small noise distorts the data, the image will not be reconstructed on the receiver side efficiently. But if the information was sent with context or the features of the image that defines the context, then the image would have been reconstructed with high accuracy even with a small level of noise.

Getting the semantics in text, images, audio, or video is challenging but with the rapid advancement in the hardware of communication and AI, it is becoming possible to replace traditional communication with semantic communication.

Following are some of the benefits of using semantic communication which makes it very useful for real-time and safety-critical applications like intelligent transportation systems:

  • In semantic communication, the features are extracted, which describe the context of an image. This makes the data less prone to noise, and it is reconstructed easily on the receiver side. Moreover, if the receiver is getting more focused and relevant data, it can efficiently reconstruct the image.
  • Only extracted features or regions of interests (ROIs) are transmitted instead of the whole image to save the bandwidth requirement as it is limited in the case of ITS, especially in intra-vehicle communication.
  • For applications like traffic surveillance and monitoring, it is efficient to send only segmented portions of the image that are useful for the application. For example, why send the image of a whole car if only the registration plate is required?
  • If an image has multiple objects and these are required by different applications, then we need to transmit the whole image. But if these applications do not need the required object or ROI, then the image can be segmented and clusters can be sent according to the required priority of use. For instance, consider a scenario similar to Figure 7.12. Imagine a car's sensor capturing an image of the road that includes multiple objects. These could be a pedestrian directly in front of the car, other vehicles in an adjacent lane, and a left-turn sign located 100 meters away

Now, if the image is segmented, it does not have to send all the objects in the image to the control unit of ADS, but it can send the information related to the pedestrian in front of the car first so that the control can actuate the mechanism to stop the car. Then, it can send the segment of the signal turning yellow so that the ADS can make the decision accordingly. There is no need to send the information related to the cars coming in the adjacent lane at this very moment because cars will be stopped due to the pedestrian and then will remain in that state because of the signal turning red. So, this example clearly shows that prioritizing the segments can save the bandwidth, lower the processing, and lower the latency by removing the communication overhead in this image.

7.4 Techniques for Semantic Segmentation and Semantic Communication

By now, we have understood the concept of semantic segmentation, its importance, and its types. Now, we will discuss some of the techniques used for semantic segmentation starting from more traditional approaches and then some end-to-end methods based on deep learning. Also, we will discuss some complete communication systems based on the semantically segmented data.

7.4.1 Conventional Approaches for Semantic Segmentation

First, the images are passed through some preprocessing layer in which steps like inverse perspective transformation (IPM), image enhancement, and ROI extraction are involved [12]. The next step involves the separation of ROI from the background, which is called detection. For candidate region detection or feature extraction, histograms of gradients (HOGs) are used which represent the features of an object based on the gradients of pixel groups in all directions [13]. Maximally stable extremal regions (MSERs) can also be used for detection purposes [13,14]. Detection is followed by recognition or classification in which extracted features are labeled according to their class. Initially, recognition was done using template matching in which a database of features related to symbols was created, and then, it is compared against the unknown image [15]. As discussed, image classification using the conventional approach involves separate layers and each layer uses different algorithms. As each layer is responsible for solving a particular task, so, when any changes are made in one layer, it is difficult to determine how it will impact the whole system. Layers of image recognition using conventional layers are shown in Figure 7.13.

7.4.2 End-to-End Approaches for Semantic Segmentation

End-to-end (E2E) approaches for image classification refer to training a possibly complex learning system by a single model. These models represent the complete target system and bypass the intermediate layers usually present in traditional pipeline designs as discussed earlier. It means that E2E models can predict output directly given the input image. In literature, there are many techniques that are used for semantic segmentation, but we will discuss some of them that are used for the application of ITS. In ITS, the most important task is environment perception which uses the classification of objects in images such as pedestrians, other vehicles, signals, road lanes, and symbolic road markings (SRMs). So, we will discuss mainly those methods which can be used for semantic segmentation.

7.4.3 SegNet – A Deep Convolutional Encoder-Decoder Architecture for Image Segmentation

SegNet [16] consists of a combination of encoder and decoder. This technique is focused on the road scene perception and tested on the CamVid road scene dataset. Consider the architecture of SegNet in Figure 7.14.

The encoder is identical to the convolution layer of VGG16 except that it has no fully connected layer, but the purpose is the same, i.e., the production of feature maps. It is the decoder and its implementation technique that distinguishes SegNet from other semantic segmentation techniques. The decoder recovers the loss of spatial information that is produced due to the pooling layers of the encoder using features from the pooling layers of the encoder; as a result, it enhances the boundary delineation, decreases the number of training parameters, and can be used in any encoder-decoder configuration with few changes. To show the true performance of the algorithm, authors have used controlled benchmarking and kept the stochastic gradient descent (SGD) throughout their end-to-end training, but this has increased the model vs optimization issues due to the gradient backpropagation issues and non-convergence nature of optimization for a large number of parameters, thus giving an accuracy of 57.7% and inference time of 60 ms.

7.4.4 ENet: A Deep Neural Network Architecture for Real-time Semantic Segmentation

ENET [17] focuses on the real-time implementation of the semantic segmentation of road scenes for high-speed inference. This technique is tested on a cityscapes dataset, giving an accuracy of 58.3%, a capacity of 79 fps, and an inference time of 13 ms which is improved as compared to SegNet. This technique uses the encoder/decoder technique but without post-processing steps, smaller decoder size, and up-sampling at the decoder using the indices of max pooling elements which makes it faster by minimizing the memory requirements. But to reduce the number of floating-point functions, it is observed that during non-linear operations, inference time increases due to increased memory calls.

7.4.5 FCN: End-to-End Deep Neural Network

Long et al. [18] propose an end-to-end training method for pixel-wise classification keeping the input and output size equal. This enables the algorithm to take the arbitrary size of the input image instead of the fixed size which enhances the accuracy of real-time implementation. Consider the architecture of FCN in Figure 7.15.

An FCN utilizes an encoder and a decoder to produce feature maps retaining spatial information using skip connections passing low-layer feature information to higher layers. This technique is tested on PASCAL VOC giving the accuracy of 65.3%, 2 fps, and 500 ms inference time. But in VGG16-based FCN, if the number of strides is reduced to get finer information during up-sampling, the size of the fully convolutional filter is increased significantly, which not only increases the computational cost but also makes it difficult to learn such large filters which affect the overall performance of this method. Also, during learning, the problem of gradient vanishing causes a loss in feature information. This technique covers the loss of feature information due to pooling layers but not due to the gradient vanishing which saturates the accuracy [19].

7.4.6 FRNN: Full Resolution of Residual Networks with Skip Connections

The solution of the problem of gradient vanishing is discussed in He et al. [19] which is caused when the number of layers is increased. The proposed method uses residual networks which transfer feature information from lower layers to higher layers. Pohlen et al. [20] has utilized the power of residual networks for the semantic segmentation of road scenes and solves not only the problem of spatial perspective loss but also the problem of gradient vanishing, thus increasing the dense prediction and localization accuracy. This method is called full resolution residual networks (FRNN) and introduces two elements in the pipeline, one method is a residual element that performs residual operations to keep the spatial resolution and the other element is the pooling element, which performs convolutions and pooling to get the feature maps. This algorithm is tested on the cityscapes dataset giving an accuracy of 71.8%. However, this technique is not suitable for real-time applications as it has an inference time of 496 ms.

7.4.7 Dilation 10

A deep neural network introduced by Yu and Koltun [21] employs a modified convolutional architecture that eliminates pooling and subsampling layers, thereby preserving the spatial resolution of the input image in the output. Dilated convolutions are used which aid the exponential growth of receptive fields, avoiding the loss of resolution. This module is used with existing dense prediction algorithms and is tested on many datasets of urban scenes like CamVid, KITTI, and cityscapes datasets with the corresponding accuracies of 65.3%, 59.2%, and 67.1%. This algorithm is not a complete pipeline for semantic segmentation but a single module that can be embedded in existing pipelines; therefore, this solution does not provide end-to-end training.

7.4.8 LDFNet

A deep neural network that increases computational efficiency [22] combines the depth and luminous information along with Red, Green and Blue (RGB) data to increase the computational efficiency by reducing the number of parameters. Table 7.1 shows how much it reduces the number of parameters when compared with other RGB techniques and increases in speed due to a reduction in the number of parameters. Comparison of LDFNet with some RGB techniques shows a substantial drop in the number of parameters, thus increasing the inference speed feature maps with a minimum number of parameters and uses Depth and Luminous (D&Y) encoders to improve the balance between accuracy and speed. This network is tested on the cityscapes dataset with an accuracy of 71.3% and a speed of 18.4 fps. This technique is very unique as compared to many existing methods as they only utilize RGB information. Testing this method on cityscapes only limits the performance as this dataset does not have accurate disparity images [23].

Table 7.1 Comparison of LDFNet with Other Neural Networks for Semantic Segmentation
Method Parameter Speed (FPS)
Dilation 10 148.8M 0.25
FCN-8s 134.5M 2.0
SegNet 29.5M 16.7
LDFNet 2.31M 18.4

Above-discussed techniques are some of the ways to segment the image semantically. Now, let’s discuss some of the important methods for the implementation of semantic communication.

7.4.9 Method 1 [24]

A typical communication system has three components.

  1. A transmitter
  2. Channel
  3. Receiver

An image is transmitted by converting the image into a binary stream at the transmitter stage. It is then transmitted over a communication channel which can be wired or wireless. Figure 7.16 shows the architecture for this technique. The receiver then reconstructs the image. As mentioned earlier, this method is more prone to noise, and the image is not reconstructed properly at the receiver. On the other hand, in this method, the elements of communication are also three, but the transmitted semantic segmentation is used for feature extraction, and then, these features are transmitted through the communication channel. Figure 7.16 shows the communication system for transmitting the image using semantic segmentation.

The first part is the transmitter which has a feature extraction block using a semantic segmentation technique, a semantic encoder, and a compression block. For semantic segmentation, a method called U-Net [25] is used. This is a CNN that can perform pixel-wise labeling of objects in an image. It has one encoder and one decoder. Each of these has different layers like convolution, maxpool, and rectified liner unit (ReLU). The encoder downsamples the image and gets the features or the context of different objects in the image. On the other hand, the purpose of the decoder is to up-sample the image and fuse the extracted features to reconstruct the image with pixel-wise labeled information. Our objective is to get the ROI and region of non-interest (RONI) from the image. Therefore, two U-Net models are used. So, the semantic segmentation block gives pixel-wise labeled images, semantic encoder 1 gives ROI, and semantic encoder 2 gives RONI. At this stage, there is one issue and that is the use of convolution layers. The size of the image reduces when it passes through the convolution layer. In semantic communication, it is important to keep the size of the input and output the same. Therefore, we have used zero-padding to perform the same convolution and it resolves our issue. The semantic decoder is used on the receiver side to reconstruct the image using the information of ROI and RONI.

On the transmitter side, after getting the ROI and RONI, the features are transmitted to the receiver through a communication channel. Data from encoder 1 and encoder 2 will be transmitted simultaneously, but the data is sparse and most of the region has black pixels or 0 values which increases the communication overhead. So, it is important to compress the data before transmitting it. The padding for keeping the size of the image the same after convolution produces a sparse matrix that contains mostly black pixels. To compress this data, sparse matrix compression is used in this method. The communication overhead is significantly reduced using this approach as it will select only important pixels or non-black pixels for transmission. Here, we can see the benefits of semantic communication discussed previously.

Now, the data is ready for transmission using a communication channel. Researchers in this method have simulated the channel instead of using a physical channel. In simulation, a communication channel is perfect because there are no real-time issues present in the channel. The major element that makes simulation different from real-time channels is noise. So, to observe the effects of noise in the channel, this method uses “additive white Gaussian noise” or AWGN. Also, the Rayleigh fading channel is used to simulate the effects of multipath propagation on the signals. Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) is used to measure the performance of the communication channel.

The receiver also has three elements as the transmitter, but the purpose is different. When data is received, data restoration is performed for decompression. After that, data is passed through the semantic decoder, which reconstructs the two parts of the image and then these two parts are fused to make the final image. The performance of this algorithm is measured through SNR and compression ratio at the receiver side.

7.4.10 Method 2 [26]

In the previous method, only high-level information was transmitted. However, an image can have multi-level information or features. A high-level feature extractor gives the overall context or meaning of an image while a low-level feature extractor investigates more details. For example, in Figure 7.10, a high-level feature will show that a meeting is going on in the image or any text, but low-level details will include that people are happy, the colors of clothes, or the texture of clothes.

The method under discussion is also used for transmitting images using multi-level semantic information for efficient bandwidth utilization. This is done mainly by reducing the communication overhead, but reducing the information can cause loss of information as well. That is why this method is designed to extract features from an image on multiple levels. More specifically, it extracts high-level features such as text and segment-based meaning and low-level features such as shapes, texture, or color information in the image. The multi-level semantic communication can be seen in Figure 7.17.

This semantic communication system also uses an encoder and decoder approach. The encoder extracts the multi-level features from an input image (S), encodes the data into symbols, and then transmits it over a noisy channel. The decoder, at the receiver side, extracts features from the symbols and then reconstructs the image (S′) using this information. The method starts by normalizing the image pixel values between 0 and 1. Three different neural networks are used for extracting different levels of features. For getting semantic information in the form of text, CaptionNet [27] based on ResNet-152 [28] and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) [29] is used. This network produces captions or text embedding based on the high-level features in the image. The segmentation-based features are extracted using the pyramid scene parsing network (PSPNet) [30]. For extracting low-level information, the concatenation technique is used to combine the normalized image and segment-based features. The encoder converts this multi-level information and transmits it over a communication channel. The channel has AWGN noise like method one. Once the data is received on the receiver, a decoder is used to remove the noise and extract the multi-level feature information. This data is then fused to reconstruct the images.

7.4.11 Method 3 [31]

In the previous two methods, it is mentioned that the reconstruction of images transmitted using semantic segmentation is of good quality. But in today’s world of image sharing, especially on mobile networks, a communication system demands transmission, reception, and reconstruction of high-quality images. This method not only implements semantic communication but also ensures that high-quality images are reconstructed at the receiver end. This method applies end-to-end semantic communication which consists of a transmitter, channel, and receiver. It also uses an encoder and decoder approach. This system is GAN (generative adversarial network) which produces or generates new images based on the provided data. It is used at the receiver end. The basic functionality of this GAN-based semantic communication system is shown in Figure 7.18.

On the transmitter side, a semantic segmentation map of an image is made using a pre-trained network on the common objects in context (COCO) dataset [32]. Note that, the GAN at the receiver is also pre-trained on the same dataset. This means that the dataset is the knowledge base for the transmitter and receiver. After converting the image into a segmented map, channel encoding is applied using a binary phase-shift keying (BPSK) encoding scheme. This data is transmitted through a noisy channel (AWGN) and is detected on the receiver. The receiver first performs the channel decoding. It then inputs the data into GAN, which uses the shared knowledge base and received data to construct the real-time high-quality image. The compression ratio of 20 is achieved using this technique which shows a good performance.

7.5 Conclusion

Transmitting an image is costly in terms of bandwidth. In many systems, such as ADS, not all the information from an image is required. Sending a complete image has communication overhead which increases the bandwidth utilization and latency. Moreover, the images in the communication systems are transmitted in the form of bits which represent all the pixels in an image. This information is prone to noise and reconstruction of images as the receiver is not accurate. In this chapter, we have presented a technique called semantic segmentation which segments the objects in an image. Using this method, the context of the picture can also be understood. Using this technique, only required information can be transmitted with reduced communication overhead, and also the information about context or semantics can help in the efficient reconstruction of an image on the receiver.

8.2 Literature Review

This section serves as a captivating journey through the diverse landscape of optimization techniques, offering a panoramic view of problem-solving approaches that have shaped the field of optimization over the years. We peer into a kaleidoscope of methodologies, ranging from classic deterministic algorithms to cutting-edge metaheuristics, each with its unique strengths and limitations. As we traverse this rich tapestry of optimization approaches, we are drawn toward the radiant glow of the MFO algorithm, standing out as a gem that beckons us with promises of novel perspectives and transformative insights into global optimization.

The symphony of optimization techniques begins with the classic elegance of deterministic algorithms, such as gradient-based methods and Newton’s method. These algorithms showcase precision and efficiency in solving well-defined problems with smooth objective functions. However, their reliance on gradient information makes them susceptible to getting trapped in local optima, limiting their effectiveness in handling complex, multimodal, and noisy optimization landscapes.

As our gaze shifts, we encounter the allure of evolutionary algorithms, such as genetic algorithms (GA) and differential evolution (DE) [1]. These algorithms mimic the principles of natural selection and evolution, exploring diverse regions of the search space and exhibiting remarkable robustness in handling complex, multimodal problems. Nevertheless, the computational burden of evaluating a large population of solutions and the challenge of balancing exploration and exploitation remain noteworthy considerations.

The symphony crescendos as we immerse ourselves in the world of metaheuristics, where innovation flourishes through a delicate balance of exploration and exploitation. Particle swarm optimization (PSO) and ant colony optimization (ACO) [2,3] showcase the power of collective intelligence, inspired by the foraging behavior of birds and ants. The emergent collaboration in these algorithms propels them toward promising solutions. However, the lack of global convergence guarantees and the vulnerability to premature convergence warrant careful scrutiny.

As the symphony reaches its zenith, we find ourselves captivated by the mesmerizing dance of swarm intelligence, where the harmony of exploration and exploitation resounds with profound implications. The charm of the firefly algorithm (FA) [4] lies in its flickering glow, where fireflies communicate through light signals to attract each other, reflecting the essence of attraction in optimization. The harmony of harmony search enchants us [5], as it draws inspiration from the improvisational nature of musical harmony, where solutions harmoniously cooperate to reach optimal states. However, these algorithms, while captivating, also have their limitations, such as the need for fine-tuning parameters and vulnerability to premature convergence in high-dimensional problems.

Amidst this symphony of optimization strategies, the radiant glow of moth flame optimization captures our attention [6]. MFO’s allure emanates from its ingenious combination of moth-inspired strategies, accentuated by the ethereal dance of flames. Drawing inspiration from the moths’ unyielding attraction to luminous sources, MFO fuses nature’s brilliance with human ingenuity, creating an algorithm that explores the search space, converges on optimal solutions, and exhibits remarkable robustness in handling various optimization challenges.

Our journey through the literature paves the way to comprehend MFO’s uniqueness and sparks the curiosity to explore its potential in solving real-world optimization problems. As we approach MFO, we are inspired to embrace the elegance of nature’s strategies and witness how this heuristic illuminates the paths to global optimization triumph [7], transcending the confines of conventional approaches. With a deeper appreciation of the optimization landscape and the radiant brilliance of MFO, we step forward into the heart of our research, ready to explore the captivating depths of this nature-inspired algorithm.

8.3 Methodology

8.3.1 Optimizing Signal Processing and AI in Optical Communication Systems: A Modified Moth Flame Optimization (MFO) Algorithm

In the MFO algorithm, several fundamental concepts are utilized to mimic the behavior of moths and optimize solutions. Let’s explain these concepts:

  1. Moth Population (M): The moth population refers to a collection of moths represented by an N × D matrix called M, where N is the number of moths, and D is the dimensionality of the solution vector that each moth represents. These moths are the search agents exploring the solution space to find optimal solutions to the given optimization problem.
  2. Flame Matrix (F): The flame matrix F is also an N × D matrix, the same size as M, used to approximate the best solutions found so far. It represents the positions of promising solutions discovered during the optimization process. In the context of MFO, “flames” act as beacons or attractive light sources that moths attempt to approach during the search.
  3. Flame Intensity: The flame intensity represents the quality or fitness value of the corresponding solutions in the flame matrix F. Each flame has an associated fitness value, which quantifies how good the solution represented by that flame is with respect to the optimization problem. The higher the flame intensity, the better the solution is considered.
  4. Attraction Behavior: The central concept of MFO is the attraction behavior of moths toward flames (optimal solutions). Inspired by the phototaxis behavior of moths moving toward light, the moths in MFO attempt to approach the flames in the flame matrix F. The closer a moth gets to a flame, the more it is influenced by the flame’s information, guiding it toward potentially better solutions.
  5. Logarithmic Spiral Movement: To model the movement of moths toward flames, the MFO algorithm uses a logarithmic spiral formula. This formula describes the trajectory of a moth as it approaches the corresponding flame. The logarithmic spiral movement pattern helps the moths converge toward flames while maintaining a balance between exploration and exploitation.

  6. Exploration and Exploitation: Exploration refers to the process of searching the solution space widely to discover new and unexplored regions. On the other hand, exploitation involves intensively focusing on already promising regions to find better solutions. MFO balances exploration and exploitation by adjusting the number of flames over iterations and guiding moths toward flames in a controlled manner. The flow diagram of the MFO algorithm is shown in Figure 8.1.

8.3.1.1 Proposed Algorithm

The flow diagram of the proposed algorithm is shown in Figure 8.2, and proceed according to the following steps:

Step 1. Initialization:

  • Set the parameters N (number of moths), D (dimensionality of the solution vector), max_iterations (maximum number of iterations), max_flames (maximum number of flames), and b (user parameter).
  • Initialize the moth population matrix M with N moths, each with a randomly generated solution vector.
  • Initialize the flame matrix F and the corresponding fitness values vector OF with the best solutions found so far. Initially, set F and OF as empty.

Step 2. Optimization Loop:

  • Repeat for each iteration l from 1 to max_iterations:
  • Compute the fitness values for each moth in M and store them in vector OM.
  • Update the flame matrix F and its corresponding fitness values vector OF:
  • For each moth in M:
  • If F is not full (less than max_flames), add the current moth and its fitness value to F and OF, respectively.
  • If F is full, compare the fitness value of the current moth (OM[i]) with the fitness value of the worst flame (OF[worst_flame_index]). If OM[i] is better, replace the worst flame with the current moth in F and update OF accordingly.
  • Update the number of flames (n) for the next iteration using equation (3): n = max_flames − (max_flames − 1) * l/max_iterations
  • Update the moths in relation to the best corresponding flames in F:
  • For each moth in M:
  • Choose the best corresponding flame in F based on fitness values similarity or other criteria.
  • Update the position of the moth using the logarithmic spiral formula: x_new = x_old + exp(−b * t) * cos(2 * pi * t) * (x_old − x_best_flame) y_new = y_old + exp(−b * t) * sin(2 * pi * t) * (y_old − y_best_flame) (where (x_old, y_old) is the current position of the moth, (x_best_flame, y_best_flame) is the position of the best corresponding flame, and t is a random variable that decreases the range of the spiral over time).

Step 3. Convergence Check:

  • Check if the termination criteria are met (e.g., the solution reaches an acceptable fitness level, or the maximum number of iterations is reached). If yes, stop the optimization process; otherwise, continue to the next iteration.

Step 4. Output

  • The optimal solution vector is found in the flame matrix F.

8.3.1.2 Key Components:

The MFO algorithm is a nature-inspired metaheuristic optimization algorithm that aims to find optimal solutions to optimization problems. Comparing the updated version provided with the existing version mentioned in the initial description, here are some potential advantages and improvements:

  1. Enhanced Exploration and Exploitation: The updated version introduces a dynamic adaptation of the number of flames over iterations. By gradually decreasing the number of flames, the algorithm shifts its focus from exploration (using more flames) to exploitation (using fewer flames). This promotes better balance between exploring the search space and exploiting promising regions, which can lead to better convergence to global optima.
  2. Improved Convergence: The use of the logarithmic spiral formula for updating the moths’ positions allows for more effective exploration near the flames and convergence toward the best solutions. The spiral-based movement patterns enable moths to follow a smooth trajectory toward the flames, which can enhance convergence speed.
  3. Automatic Flame Management: The update for the number of flames based on the current iteration allows the algorithm to adaptively adjust its population size. This feature prevents the algorithm from being trapped in local optima by reducing the number of flames over time and focusing on the most promising regions.
  4. Efficient Memory of Best Solutions: The algorithm maintains a flame matrix F to store the best solutions found so far. This memory mechanism helps in retaining promising solutions across iterations and facilitates better exploitation of valuable information.
  5. Tunable Parameters: The introduction of user-defined parameters (e.g., b) in the logarithmic spiral formula provides greater flexibility to fine-tune the algorithm’s behavior according to the problem characteristics.
  6. Simplicity and Compactness: The algorithm is relatively compact and straightforward to implement compared to some complex metaheuristic algorithms, making it easy to understand and use.

However, it’s important to note that the effectiveness of the updated version of the MFO algorithm may still heavily depend on various factors, such as problem domain, parameter tuning, and the nature of the optimization problem itself. There is no one-size-fits-all optimization algorithm, and the performance of MFO should be evaluated on a case-by-case basis or compared against other state-of-the-art optimization methods to determine its superiority for specific problem instances. Additionally, performance comparisons should consider factors such as convergence speed, solution quality, and computational efficiency.

8.3.1.3 Pseudocode

The pseudocode of the proposed approach is provided below:

Function MothFlameOptimization(N, D, max_iterations, max_flames, b):

  • Initialize moth population matrix M with N moths, each with a randomly generated solution vector
  • Initialize flame matrix F and its corresponding fitness values vector OF as empty

For l = 1 to max_iterations:

  • // Step 1: Update Flames
  • Compute the fitness values for each moth in M and store them in vector OM

If F is not full (less than max_flames):

  • Add the best moth and its fitness value to F and OF

Else:

  • Find the index of the worst flame in F (worst_flame_index)

For each moth i in M:

  • If OM[i] is better than OF[worst_flame_index]:
  • Replace the worst flame with moth i and update OF accordingly
  • // Step 2: Update the number of flames

n = max_flames - ((max_flames - 1) * l) / max_iterations

  • Step 3: Update Moths
  • For each moth i in M:
    • Select the best corresponding flame in F based on fitness value similarity or other criteria
    • Generate a random value t between 0 and 1
  • For d = 1 to D:
    • Update the position of the moth’s d-th dimension using the logarithmic spiral formula:
      • x_new = x_old + exp(-b * t) * cos(2 * pi * t) * (x_old - x_best_flame[d])
      • y_new = y_old + exp(-b * t) * sin(2 * pi * t) * (y_old - y_best_flame[d])
      • Update the solution vector of moth i using the new position (x_new, y_new)
    • Return the best solution vector found in the flame matrix F
  • End Function

8.3.1.4 Parameter Settings and Dynamic Adaptation

Static Parameter Settings:

Traditionally, MFO has relied upon static parameter settings, wherein the key algorithmic parameters, such as the population size, the maximum flight distance, and the attraction factors, remain steadfast throughout the optimization endeavor.

Dynamic Parameter Integration:

To infuse MFO with an added edge and elevate its performance, researchers have begun to explore dynamic parameter adaptation strategies. By infusing the algorithm with dynamic parameters, capable of adapting in real time based on the optimization progress and problem characteristics, the optimization process gains newfound agility, bolstering convergence speed and augmenting its robustness in conquering complex optimization landscapes.

8.4 Applications of MFO

8.4.1 Real-Life Problem-Solving

The enchanting allure of MFO extends well beyond theoretical conjectures, as it has already found a potent place in real-world optimization problems. These captivating applications span diverse domains, transcending the boundaries of machine learning, engineering, and finance. MFO has adorned itself with the mantle of a potent problem.

8.4.2 Application of MFO in Disaster Response Planning

8.4.2.1 Problem Statement

Disaster response planning involves coordinating emergency services, resources, and personnel to effectively respond to natural disasters, such as earthquakes, hurricanes, floods, and wildfires. Optimizing disaster response planning is critical for efficiently allocating resources, minimizing response times, and saving lives. However, disaster response planning is a complex and dynamic problem, with uncertainties in disaster severity, location, and timing.

8.4.2.2 How MFO Can Help

MFO’s ability to strike a balance between exploration and exploitation makes it well-suited for the dynamic and uncertain nature of disaster response planning. The algorithm can explore different resource allocation strategies and evacuation plans while exploiting successful solutions to optimize key disaster response performance metrics.

8.4.3 Application of MFO in Supply Chain Optimization

8.4.3.1 Problem Statement

Supply chain management involves the coordination of various activities, resources, and stakeholders to ensure the smooth flow of goods and services from suppliers to end consumers. Optimizing supply chain operations is essential for minimizing costs, reducing lead times, improving customer satisfaction, and enhancing overall efficiency. However, supply chain optimization is a complex and dynamic problem due to the involvement of multiple interconnected processes and uncertainties in demand, supply, and transportation.

8.4.3.2 How MFO Can Help

MFO’s ability to balance exploration and exploitation makes it suitable for tackling the multidimensional and dynamic nature of supply chain optimization. The algorithm can explore different supply chain configurations and decision-making strategies while exploiting successful solutions to optimize key supply chain performance metrics.

8.4.3.3 Future Research

Future research in this area could focus on integrating real-time data and situational awareness into the optimization model, exploring multi-objective optimization to balance conflicting disaster response objectives and considering community-level engagement in disaster planning. Additionally, exploring hybrid approaches that combine MFO with machine learning techniques for demand forecasting and resource allocation could further improve disaster response planning.

Future research in this area could focus on incorporating additional factors into the optimization model, such as sustainability considerations, supplier performance, and demand forecasting. Moreover, exploring hybrid approaches that combine MFO with machine learning techniques for demand prediction and supply chain risk management could further enhance the effectiveness of supply chain optimization.

The MFO algorithm has shown promising capabilities in various optimization scenarios, but its journey is far from over. As researchers continue to push the boundaries of metaheuristics, several intriguing avenues of future research emerge to further enhance and explore the potential of MFO. Some potential areas of future research include:

Advanced Parameter Adaptation: Investigating more sophisticated and adaptive parameter settings for MFO could lead to improved performance in handling complex and dynamic optimization landscapes. Dynamic parameter adaptation strategies could be explored to enable MFO to dynamically adjust its parameters during the optimization process, enhancing its responsiveness and efficiency.

Hybridization with Other Algorithms: Exploring hybridization with other optimization algorithms could be fruitful in creating novel and powerful optimization approaches. Combining the strengths of MFO with those of other metaheuristics, such as GAs, PSO, or ant colony optimization, could result in hybrid algorithms with enhanced global search capabilities.

Multi-Objective Optimization: Extending MFO to address multi-objective optimization problems is an exciting avenue for future research. Developing a multi-objective variant of MFO that can efficiently find a set of Pareto-optimal solutions would cater to a wide range of real-world applications with conflicting objectives.
