chapter 9Optical Communication and Signal Process with AI

Sonam Gour, Priya Chaudhary, and Amit Rathi

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472506-9

9.1 Introduction

The main aim of optical antennas is nanoscale light control and manipulation. So, another name of antenna is nano-antenna or optical nano-antenna. They are very comparable to radiofrequency antennas even though they operate in the optical or near-infrared regions of the electromagnetic spectrum. Noble metals such as gold, silver, or aluminum are commonly used for these antennas because of their intense light interaction at the nanoscale, which is attributed to their outstanding plasmonic properties [1]. By concentrating electromagnetic fields into minuscule spaces, they facilitate optical nanoscale manipulation at the nanoscale and entrapment of particles in optical antennas for enhancing the sensitivity of sensors and boosting the performance of photodetectors.

Focusing the light into subwavelength volumes is used as a main characteristic of optical antennas. It allows light to be manipulated beyond the diffraction limit. This feature has enabled applications in spectroscopy, nanoscale imaging, and the development of novel optoelectronic devices [2]. Main point for the research of the optical antenna is the focus on improving their bandwidth, efficiency, and integration with nanophotonic components for varieties of applications in imaging, telecommunications, sensing, and quantum information processing.

9.2 Design Requirements of Optical Antenna

Various applications are the deciding parameters for the design of optical antennas. Some major applications are discussed in the following section:

9.2.1 Plasmonic Properties

The choice of material affects the antenna’s plasmonic properties, including resonance frequency and field enhancement. Nobel metals like gold, silver, or aluminium are used for designing the optical antenna.

9.2.2 Geometry

The antenna’s size, shape, and component arrangement determine a lot of its optical characteristics. Nanorods, nanodiscs, bowtie antennas, and nanoapertures are examples of common shapes; each offers special benefits for certain uses.

9.2.3 Resonance Frequency

The frequencies at which optical antennas resonate match the wavelengths of light that they are intended to interact with. To enhance the efficacy of light absorption, scattering, or coupling, the resonance frequency must be modified [3].

9.2.4 Polarisation Sensitivity

An antenna’s optical response may be impacted by the polarisation of incident light. For some applications, polarisation-selective qualities might be useful, but for others, polarisation-insensitive behaviour might be required in the antenna design.

9.2.5 Efficiency

The goal of optical antennas is to effectively transform incident light into desired optical processes, like near-field enhancement, absorption, or scattering. Reducing losses from radiation damping, absorption in the metal, and other non-radiative processes is necessary to maximise efficiency.

9.2.6 Near-Field Enhancement

The main use of antenna is concentration towards electromagnetic fields with subwavelength region in the near filed region. It is essential for spectroscopy, nonlinear optics, and sensing applications.

9.2.7 Compatibility with Fabrication

The material of choice, geometry of antenna, and specifications of substrate should all work with the fabrication method. Electron beam lithography technique focused on template-assisted and beam milling. Nanofabrication is frequently used for creating nanoscale optical antennas [4].

9.2.8 Integration

It may be necessary to connect with optical antennas for optical components like photodetectors, waveguides, or emitters in order for constructing useful devices. Besides the considerations of integration, they are used for coupling efficiency, alignment accuracy, and compatibility with existing fabrication procedures.

9.2.9 Environmental Stability

A number of factors are considered, including as humidity and temperature. The material surrounding the antenna could impact the optical characteristics of the antenna. Stability in right environmental conditions is required for a gadget to function reliably.

9.2.10 Scalability

For practical applications, optical antennas required to be scalable in size, inexpensive in manufacture, and compatible for large-scale production techniques.

All objectives must be considered with a multidisciplinary approach with combining ideas such as optics, nanofabrication, materials science, and device engineering is often required.

9.3 Advantages of Optical Antenna

Optical antennas are advantageous in various applications because they give a number of benefits compared to the traditional optical structure and components. Some main advantages are as follows:

9.3.1 Nanoscale Light Confinement

By concentrating light into subwavelength volumes, optical antennas can significantly strengthen the surrounding electromagnetic fields. This property enhances nonlinear optical effects, photodetection efficiency, and sensor sensitivity by permitting more light-matter interactions at the nanoscale.

9.3.2 Plasmonic Resonances

Optical antennas utilise the plasmonic resonances of noble metal nanostructures, which can be tuned to match specific light wavelengths. These resonances are helpful for wavelength-selective filtering, surface-enhanced spectroscopy, and display colour tuning because they enable light to be selectively absorbed, scattered, or amplified at particular frequencies.

9.3.3 Enhanced Light Harvesting

Optical antennas have the potential to enhance light harvesting in photovoltaic and photodetection devices by directing incident light towards active regions such as semiconductor junctions or quantum dots. Among the advantages of this improvement in terms of device performance are higher photocurrent production and sensitivity.

9.3.4 Subwavelength Imaging

Optical antennas enable subwavelength imaging techniques that surpass the diffraction limit of conventional optics. The light is directed towards subwavelength nanostructures or apertures; optical antennas are used for achieving resolution that is greater than that limited by the wavelength of light themselves. It makes possible for image features and structures at the nanoscale with high resolution [5].

9.3.5 Tailored Optical Characteristics

Optical qualities of an optical antenna can be adjusted by modifying its shape, size, and material composition. It is used in a range of frequencies such as optical trapping, biosensing, and plasmonic catalysis due to their tunability. Antennas with tunable polarisation sensitivity, optical responses, and spectrum selectivity can be tailored for a range of applications,

9.3.6 Flexibility in Integration

Waveguides, photonic circuits, and imaging platforms can be utilised with optical antennas with a variety of optical components and systems. Their compatibility with current nanofabrication processes can be used seamlessly with intricate devices and systems.

9.3.7 Dynamic Control

Optical antennas can display dynamic optical responses because they are actively controlled by exposing it into external stimuli like electric fields, temperature variations, or optical excitation [6]. With applications in reconfigurable optics and optoelectronics, this dynamic tunability makes possible reconfigurable optical systems, switchable plasmonic resonances, and adaptive meta surfaces.

9.3.8 Versatility

Applications for optical antennas can be found in a wide range of industries, such as quantum sensing, technologies, spectroscopy, imaging, and telecommunications. Because of their adaptability and multipurpose nature, they are useful components for creating cutting-edge optical systems and devices that are customised to meet particular application needs.

All things considered, optical antennas’ special qualities and capacities make them intriguing options for expanding the field of nanophotonic and facilitating innovations across a range of technical fields.

9.4 Physical Property of Biomedical Antenna

Although optical antennas and their radio frequency (RF) and microwave counterparts are very similar, there are important distinctions in their scaling behaviour and physical characteristics. The majority of these variations result from the fact that metals are strongly correlated plasmas that are referred to as free electron gases rather than ideal conductors at optical frequencies. Galvanic drives are also not commonly used to drive optical antennas.

Through the use of an optical antenna, a transmitter or receiver can interact with free optical radiation. An elemental quantum absorber or emitter, such as an atom, ion, molecule, quantum dot, or defect centre in a solid, is the optimal form for the receiver or transmitter [7].

The interaction between the radiation field and the emitter or absorber is improved by the antenna. Consequently, it offers the possibility of managing the interaction between light and matter at the level of a single quantum system. The receiver/transmitter’s transition rates and, in the event of a significant interaction, even the energy-level structure are altered by the antenna’s existence as shown in Figure 9.1. Similar to how the receiver-transmitter characteristics affect the antenna properties, it becomes clear that the two need to be thought of as a linked system.

9.5 Optical Fibre-Based Antenna for Biomedical Applications

Because of their high sensitivity, low invasiveness, and compatibility with biological surroundings, optical fibre-based antennas have special benefits for biomedical applications. The following are some benefits and possible uses:

9.5.1 Biocompatibility

Optical fibres can come into close touch with biological tissues and fluids since they are usually composed of biocompatible materials like silica. Because of its biocompatibility, less invasive sensing and imaging methods can be used, which lessens tissue damage and patient discomfort [8].

9.5.2 Miniaturisation

The creation of minuscule antennas for targeted distribution to certain biological areas is made possible by the ability to produce optical fibres with diameters as small as a few micrometres. Miniaturisation is highly beneficial for applications that need precise spatial resolution and localisation, such as targeted drug delivery and in vivo imaging.

9.5.3 Telemedicine

The ability of optical fibre antennas to transmit and receive optical signals over great distances allows for the remote detection and observation of biological activity taking place far inside the body. This capability is invaluable in scenarios such as endoscopic imaging were large, traditional antennas may be obtrusive or impractical.

9.5.4 Multiplexing

Many physiological characteristics or biomarkers can be simultaneously imaged and sensed by integrating many optical fibres into a single probe or catheter. Multiplexing enhances the information content and diagnostic capabilities of optical fibre-based antennas, enabling comprehensive biomedical evaluations.

9.5.5 Extremely Perceptive

Because optical fibres are highly sensitive to variations in optical properties such as refractive index, absorption, and scattering, they are perfect for label-free biomolecule and cellular process detection [9]. Target analytes can be selectively collected by functionalising optical fibre antennas with specific ligands or receptors, enabling the sensitive detection of infections and disease biomarkers.

9.5.6 Live Observation

Optical fibre antennas give continuous monitoring of patient’s state and their progression for illness by giving real-time input for physiological signs. Real-time monitoring is particularly helpful when it comes to dynamic processes where rapid response is necessary for patient care, such as medication metabolism, tissue perfusion, and cerebral activity.

9.5.7 Imaging at High Resolution

Utilising optical fibre antennas in conjunction with state-of-the-art imaging modalities such as multiphoton microscopy, optical coherence tomography (OCT), and confocal microscopy allows for high-resolution imaging of biological tissues and structures. The capacity of high-resolution imaging to offer accurate investigation of cellular shape, tissue architecture, and disease pathology supports early diagnosis and therapy planning. Besides being utilised for sensing and imaging, optical fibre antennas can also be employed for therapeutic applications like photodynamic therapy and phototherapy.

Optical fibre antennas have the potential to be useful in the treatment of cancer and the management of infections because they can selectively ablate tumours, eradicate infections, and activate biological processes by directing light energy directly to target areas. All things considered, optical fibre-based antennas are quite promising for a variety of biomedical uses, such as monitoring, treatments, and diagnostics. It is anticipated that further study and development in this area will produce creative answers to problems in healthcare and enhance patient outcomes.

9.6 Working Principal of Optical Fibre-Based Antenna

The transmission and reception of optical signals via the optical fibre are essential to the operation of an antenna based on optical fibre. This is how it typically operates:

9.6.1 Transmission of Optical Signals

Through total internal reflection, optical fibres function as waveguides, directing light along their length. An optical signal that is fired into one end of the fibre will travel down its core with the least amount of loss from scattering or reflection. To encode information, the signal can be modified in a number of ways, including phase modulation and intensity modulation.

9.6.2 Fibre-Optic Antenna Structure

An optical fibre coupled to a photonic or plasmonic nanostructure at the end is the usual configuration for an optical fibre-based antenna. Similar to the antenna in conventional electromagnetic systems, this nanostructure functions as the radiating element. The nanostructure can be engineered to improve the way light interacts with matter, for example, by concentrating light into subwavelength volumes or forming plasmonic resonances.

9.6.3 Light-Matter Interaction

Optical fibre lights for the antennas interact with optical characteristics of the nanostructure. This interaction leads to various effects, including scattering, enhanced light emission, or absorption, all of which depend on the design of the nanostructure. Plasmonic nanostructures can focus electromagnetic fields into nanoscale volumes by inducing surface plasmon.

9.6.4 Optical Signal Radiation

As a result of interactions between light and nanostructure, optical signals can be discharged into the environment. They generated signals, which contains modulation of the incoming light. It can be detected by external optical receivers. The efficiency and radiation pattern of the antenna depend on a number of factors, such as material composition, geometry, and excitation conditions of the nanostructure.

9.6.5 Optical Signal Receiving

The optical fibre antenna can sense incoming optical signals from the surroundings when they are operated in the receiving mode. Optical properties such as resonance shifting and intensity modulation can be changed with interactions between external optical signals and the nanostructure at the antenna’s end.

9.6.6 Design Specific to Applications

Optical fibres antenna can be used for specific applications, including sensing, imaging, and communication. Antennas for sensing applications can be adjusted for high sensitivity for changes in the environment surrounding, while antennas for imaging applications might be designed to achieve high contrast and spatial resolution.

Optical fibre antennas use nanostructures’ unique properties for transmitting and receiving the signals.

9.7 Material Design for Optical Fibre Antennas

The application, fabrication methods, desired optical qualities, and environmental compatibility are elements that determine the construction of optical fibre antennas. The following materials are frequently used for building optical fibre antennas:

9.7.1 Silica-Based Optical Fibres

Silica is used for the most widely used waveguides for optical signal transmission. They have minimal optical losses, good mechanical flexibility, and high transparency with a wide range of wavelengths for visible and near-infrared region. Germanium or phosphorus are commonly used for adding silica fibres in order to alter their optical characteristics, including dispersion and refractive index.

9.7.2 Noble Metals (Such as Gold and Silver)

Noble metals like gold and silver are frequently utilised for creating nanostructures at the end of optical fibres for plasmonic antennas. Strong surface plasmon resonances are shown by these metals, which can improve nanoscale light-matter interactions. Commonly used nanostructures for plasmonic optical fibre antennas are bowtie antennas, nanorods, nano discs, and gold and silver nanoparticles.

9.7.3 Dielectric Materials

Polymers, silicon, silicon nitride, and titanium dioxide (TiO2) are examples of dielectric materials that can be used to produce nanostructures on optical fibres [10]. Reduced optical losses, optical property tunability, and compatibility with complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor (CMOS) production techniques are some of the benefits of dielectric antennas. Optical fibres can be combined with dielectric nanostructures like waveguides, photonic crystals, and dielectric resonators to create tailored optical functionalities.

9.7.4 Semiconductor Materials

Silicon (Si) and gallium arsenide (GaAs) are two semiconductor materials that can be used to provide light sources (like semiconductor lasers) and photodetectors (like photodiodes) in optical fibre antennas [11]. Optical fibres and semiconductor nanostructures can be joined directly by techniques like epitaxial growth, wafer bonding, and hybrid integration.

9.7.5 Functional Coatings

To alter the optical characteristics of optical fibres or nanostructures or improve their performance for particular uses, functional coatings or layers can be put on their surface. Biomolecular coatings for biofunctionalisation and biosensing, dielectric coatings for antireflection or passivation, and photonic crystal coatings for improved light manipulation are a few examples.

9.7.6 Hybrid Materials

By combining several materials in hybrid structures, such as semiconductor-metal hybrids or metal-dielectric composites, it is possible to produce optical qualities or functions that are not possible when using the component materials alone. Hybrid optical fibre antennas can minimise the drawbacks of each component material while utilising their capabilities.

Biocompatibility (for biomedical applications), mechanical robustness, cost-effectiveness, optical performance, and compatible with fabrication processes are some of the factors that influence the selection of materials for optical fibre antennas. To maximise the performance and design of optical fibre antennas for particular applications, multidisciplinary approaches combining materials science, nanotechnology, photonics, and device engineering are frequently used.

9.8 Equipment Used for Fibre-Based Antenna

The tools required for fibre-based antennas vary depending on the kind of antenna being used and the particular application. The following is a list of typical equipment used:

9.8.1 Optical Fibre

The essential part of fibre-based antennas is optical fibres. Light signals are guided to and from the antenna construction by them. The fibres can differ in terms of material composition (plastic, silica), core diameter, and kind (single-mode, multi-mode).

9.8.2 Light Source

In order to produce optical signals that are sent through the optical fibre, a light source is needed. Depending on the needs of the application, this can comprise lasers, light-emitting diodes (LEDs), or broadband light sources.

9.8.3 Photodetector

A photodetector is a device that helps the antenna transform optical signals into electrical signals. Avalanche photodiodes (APDs), photomultiplier tubes (PMTs), and photodiodes are common forms of photodetectors.

9.8.4 Splitting and Combining

Coupling optical signals into and out of the optical fibre are the functions of optical couplers and splitters, respectively. They might be active (like optical modulators) or passive (like fibre couplers and splitters).

9.8.5 Optical Amplifiers

Optical amplifiers are used to increase the power of an optical signal, especially in applications requiring low-sensitivity detection or long-distance transmission. Two such examples are semiconductor optical amplifiers (SOAs) and erbium-doped fibre amplifiers (EDFAs).

9.8.6 Optical Modulators

They are used to change an optical signal’s polarisation, phase, or strength as it travels through an optical fibre. These could be Mach-Zehnder interferometers (MZIs), acousto-optic modulators, or electro-optic modulators.

9.8.7 Optical Filters

Optical filters are used to filter optical signals according to particular wavelengths or frequency bands. Thin-film interference filters, fibre Bragg gratings, or Fabry-Perot cavities can be used to implement them.

9.8.8 Spectrum Analysers

They are used for measuring the bandwidth, wavelength, and power level of optical signals, among other spectral characteristics. They are crucial for describing the functionality of optical systems and fibre-based antennas. Specifically made spectrum analysers for the analysis of optical signals are known as optical spectrum analysers or OSAs for short [12]. They can identify features with tiny linewidths and offer high-resolution measurements of optical spectra.

9.8.9 Optical Microscopes

The nanostructures that are created on or around the optical fibre antenna are inspected and characterised using optical microscopes. They are necessary for alignment, quality assurance, and antenna performance optimisation [13].

9.8.10 Signal Generators and Analysers

These devices are used to produce and examine electrical signals that communicate with the antenna system’s optical components [14]. They are necessary for debugging and verifying the overall functionality of the system.

9.8.11 Alignment Tools

To precisely align optical components and nanostructures within the fibre-based antenna system, alignment tools like piezoelectric stages, micromanipulators, and automated alignment systems are utilised.

These are simply a few instances of the gear that is frequently used with fibre-based antennas. Depending on the use, complexity, and performance requirements of the antenna system, different equipment will be needed.

9.9 Antenna for Optical Communication with Signal Processing

The digital signal processing deals with the modification of the optical signals after being translated from analogue to digital signal. For optical communication systems to effectively deal with the complications of light-based data transmission, signal processing is necessary. In order to improve signal quality, diminish the impact of channel difficulties, and lower noise, it includes different techniques which comprise modulation, coding-decoding, filtering, and equalisation. Signal processing provides the high data rate communication by utilising the current carrier frequencies. To improve data transfer, error correction, and overall system performance in high-speed optical communication, the signal processing handles the electric signals produced from the optical signal. Various technologies are emerged in the competition to offer ultra-fast wireless communication systems, like optical wireless communication (OWC) systems. For OWC, digital signal processing is essential to optimise data transmission efficiency and dependability in difficult circumstances, such as space, air, or underwater.

9.9.1 Optical Wireless Communication (OWC)

Optical wireless communication offers high-speed wireless data transmission across short to medium distances by using light. For data transmission across the atmosphere, OWC systems utilise an OW transmitter to modulate a beam of visible, infrared or ultra-violet light. OWC system provides a substitute for conventional RF communication and delivers solutions to radio system bandwidth constraints [15].

The near-infrared light (mainly 850, 1,310, and 1,550 nm) based antennas are very useful for data transmission in near-infrared wireless optical communication system. The OWC system transmits light using laser diodes and LEDs, comparable to fibre optic systems. Since the OWC system can work at high data rates and have a short linewidth and great efficiency, laser diodes are frequently utilised as the light source in such systems. For the laser diode as light source in antenna for near-infrared OWC, the choice of III–V semiconductor material serves as crucial, specially adapted for fibre optic communication, near-infrared spectroscopy, and biomedical applications. A few semiconductor materials such as silicon, InP, GaAs, InGaAs, GaAsSb, InPSb, and AlGaAs, are well suited for these kinds of applications and are widely used. The OWC system includes the infrared and visible communication potential approaches. The OWC system has several major types such as free space optics (FSO), underwater wireless optical communication (UWOC), and inter-satellite optical wireless communication (IsOWC), each of which offers unique benefits and opportunities.

9.9.1.1 Free Space Optics (FSO)

The free space optic transmits data via light (typically through laser) between two points with a clear line of sight. It is utilised in metropolitan areas and between buildings, where establishing a physical connection would be impractical. The FSO system is perfect for broadband access because it can travel long distances at high data rates.

9.9.1.2 Underwater Wireless Optical Communication (UWOC)

The UWOC system transmits data across water using visible light, frequently in the blue or green wavelength. Due to high attenuation or limited bandwidth, conventional techniques like radio waves are inefficient in underwater circumstances. In these situations, this technology is especially helpful. Environment monitoring, marine communication, and underwater exploration all depend on UWOC.

9.9.1.3 Inter-Satellite Optical Wireless Communication (IsOWC)

In inter-satellite OWC, laser beams are used to facilitate satellite-to-satellite communication. Deep space communication, worldwide internet coverage, and satellite networks all depend on this technology because of its exceptionally high data rates and minimal latency. In IsOWC systems, optical antennas need to be well aligned and able to resist the adverse conditions of space.

9.9.2 Role of AI in Antennas for Optical Wireless Communication

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies like deep learning, machine learning, and neural networks can be applied to the OWC system to optimise system design, resulting in improved performance, enhanced security, and real-time adaptation to changing situations.

9.9.2.1 Optimisation of Antenna Design

AI plays a crucial role in developing high-performance antennas with appropriate features for certain OWC applications. In order to optimise antenna designs, AI systems, in particular machine learning models, can examine large datasets of material qualities, design parameters, and ambient factors. Antennas with increased gain, directivity, and efficiency result from this.

9.9.2.2 Real-Time System Adaptation

AI maintains excellent communication capabilities in a variety of environmental circumstances. Real-time analysis of the communication environment by AI can allow for the modification of the system parameters in real time, including power levels, modulation schemes, and beam steering. In spite of changing situations, this implies that the optical wireless system always runs at maximum efficiency.

9.9.2.3 Adaptive Signal Processing

AI enhances the quality and reliability of data transfer in instances of difficulty. AI has the potential to improve digital signal processing through consistent adaptation to altering the communication channel conditions. AI-driven adaptive filtering ensures decreasing error rates and improved signal reception by reducing the impact of atmospheric disturbance in FSO systems and scattering in UWOC systems.

9.9.2.4 Enhanced Security

AI ensures secure data transmission. Traffic pattern can be monitored by AI, which can identify irregularities that could point to security risks like signal jamming or spying. AI has the ability to improve encryption methods, increasing communication security against online threats.

9.10 Literature Review on the Use of Optical Fibre Antennas in Biomedical

According to a review of the literature, there is an increasing amount of research being done on the possible uses of optical fibre antennas in biomedicine [16]. A fibre-optic Fabry-Perot interferometric sensor for very sensitive and accurate physiological parameter measurement, including blood pressure and temperature, was demonstrated in a study that emphasised the benefits of optical fibre sensors’ miniaturisation, biocompatibility, and multiplexing capacities for usage in biomedical applications [17]. In order to facilitate non-invasive illness diagnostics, the author created a miniature fibre-optic confocal microscope for in vivo imaging of biological tissues with subcellular resolution.

The potential of OCT for the early identification of ocular disorders was demonstrated which developed an OCT system based on fibre-optic antennas for high-resolution imaging of retinal structures and vasculature [18]. The use of optical fibre-based antennas for controlled release of therapeutic agents in localised drug delivery applications was the subject of a study by the author which demonstrated superior temporal and spatial control over drug release compared to traditional approaches.

The effectiveness of photothermal therapy for the treatment of cancer was demonstrated by the author for investigation into the use of gold-coated optical fibres as photothermal actuators for the targeted annihilation of cancer cells.

In order to detect biomolecules like DNA and proteins label-free, the authors created a fibre-optic biosensor based on plasmonic antennas that has high sensitivity and specificity for use in biomedical diagnostics [19]. In their overview of the latest developments in fibre-optic surface-enhanced Raman scattering (SERS) sensors for biomedical sensing applications, they emphasised the sensors’ promise for sensitive and quick identification of disease biomarkers.

Optogenetic manipulation of neuronal activity in deep brain areas can be achieved with fibre-optic antennas, opening up new possibilities for neurotherapeutic applications and providing precise control over neural circuits [20].

These studies highlight the increasing importance of optical antennas in a wide range of applications. The unique properties of antennas, including high sensitivity, small size, and the ability to function in biological environments, make them promising for advancing biomedical diagnosis, treatment, and research. Further research in this field is used with significant therapeutic applications.

9.11 Conclusion

Optical fibre antennas have a wide range of biological applications due to their unique capacities and properties. Optical fibre antennas have been useful in a variety of biomedical domains, drug administration, biological analysis, neural interface, sensing, and imaging. It is used for developing tiny, biocompatible devices which communicate with biological systems. They are perfect for integration with biomedical equipment and minimally invasive procedures because of adaptability and their small size. Optical fibre antennas are a sensitive and selective means of detecting cells, biomolecules, and tissues.

They enable label-free sensing with good detection limits and specificity and also use for enhancing light-matter interactions at the nanoscale. Fibre-based antennas are used in high-resolution imaging of biological tissues and structures. It is used for aiding in the early detection and diagnosis of disease. It offers fine-grained control by therapeutic effects at certain body sites over medicine delivery. It includes optogenetics, photothermal therapy, and targeted drug release, which may be used in regenerative medicine, neurotherapeutics, and cancer treatment. Real-time monitoring of physiological data and neural activity is made possible by fibre-based antennas.

Optical fibre antennas enable bidirectional communication with neural tissues. It is essential for the development of periprosthetic devices and brain machine interfaces. Integrating optical fibre antennas with other optical and electrical components are used for creating multifunctional biomedical devices with enhanced functionality and performance. Their compatibility with current fabrication techniques facilitates their easy integration into biological systems and technologies. The use of optical fibre antennas has greatly advanced biomedical research, diagnosis, and therapy. It is expected that additional research and development in that field enhances patient monitoring outcomes and expands our knowledge of complex biological systems.

chapter 10Microwave Photonic RadarIntroduction and Signal Processing Challenges

Hum Nath Parajuli

DOI: 10.1201/9781003472506-10

Radio detection and ranging (radar) systems have traditionally been used to determine the range, velocity, and imaging of target objects. Conventional radar systems rely on electronic technologies. However, recent advancements in photonic technology have generated significant interest in microwave photonics (MWP)-based radar systems. These systems provide significant benefits compared to conventional electronic radar, such as high-bandwidth and high-frequency signal generation and processing with minimal noise. Additionally, they allow reconfigurability in terms of bandwidth, chirp rate, and frequency for radar signals. This potential for advancement could transform the future of radar technology. This chapter provides an overview of MWP-based radars, emphasizing their benefits and potential for future radar systems.

Various radar signal processing techniques are essential for accurately extracting desired measurements. These techniques include waveform design tailored to specific applications; mitigation of path loss, noise, and interference; and enhancements in range, Doppler, and imaging performance. Discussing all of these signal-processing challenges is beyond the scope of this chapter. However, this chapter introduces radar-radar interference mitigation (IM) techniques for photonic radar systems, which are increasingly important as the number of radar users are growing with advancements in technology in diverse applications. This chapter addresses the interference issue by exploring two key strategies: designing interference-resistant waveforms and applying deep learning techniques to effectively suppress the effects of multi-radar interferences. These techniques hold significant potential for advancing radar technology and addressing the emerging challenges of multi-radar IM.

10.1 Introduction to Radar Systems

Radio detection and ranging (radar) sensing systems use electromagnetic waves across a wide frequency range, from long-range (10 MHz to 1 GHz) to short-range (1 GHz to mmWave), to determine the range, velocity, direction, and imaging of target objects. Depending on the operating frequency bands, which range from the HF-band (3–30 MHz) to mm-wave bands (40–300 GHz), radar applications vary [1]. The initial application of radar dates back to World War II, where it was used for military purposes to locate target objects. Since then, radar systems have been utilized in various fields, including defense, remote sensing, industry, air navigation, marine, and more recently, automotive, agriculture, and medical industries [2–7].

Radars can be categorized based on operating frequency, waveform type, and detection methods. For instance, pulse radar uses short pulses to detect target objects and is employed in military, airport, and weather monitoring applications. Continuous wave (CW) radar uses a continuous signal to measure the frequency shift of the echo signal (reflected signal from an object), which helps to determine the range and velocity of the target object. A subtype of CW radar is a frequency-modulated continuous wave (FMCW) radar, which uses a continuous waveform with linear frequency modulation (LFM) [8–10]. LFM radar is popular in automotive applications due to its excellent pulse compression characteristics, providing finer range and Doppler (velocity) resolution. Similarly, synthetic aperture radar (SAR) uses high-frequency CW signals along with antenna movement to capture high-resolution images of target objects, making it popular in remote sensing applications.

In a typical radar system, the radar transmitter generates a waveform or pulse that is directed toward target objects, and the reflected echo is captured by the radar receiver. By analyzing the properties of the received echo signal, such as amplitude, phase, frequency, and time delay, various measurable characteristics of the detected object, including range, velocity, direction, shape, and size can be determined. Various radar signal processing techniques are essential for accurately extracting these desired properties. These techniques include designing suitable waveforms tailored to specific applications; minimizing the effects of path loss, noise, and interference; and enhancing range, Doppler, and imaging measurement performance through advanced signal processing methods, including deep learning (DL) techniques.

Conventional commercial radar systems primarily rely on electronic technology. However, with recent advancements in photonic technology, there has been significant interest in developing MWP-based radar systems, especially within academic research. Due to the numerous advantages photonics offers over electronic systems, it holds substantial potential to revolutionize future radar systems. The next section provides a brief introduction to MWP-based radars.

10.2 Microwave Photonics (MWP) for Radar

MWP in radar involves the integration of photonic and microwave technologies to generate, transmit, and process microwave radar signals, typically within the frequency range of 1–100 GHz. This interdisciplinary approach leverages the advantages of photonics, such as low-loss transmission, wide bandwidth, and immunity to electromagnetic interference, to enhance radar system performance. MWP-based radar systems can operate in free space optical (FSO) channels, where they benefit from the same advantages but also suffer from similar environmental challenges as traditional FSO systems [11–13]. These challenges include atmospheric turbulence, absorption, scattering, and weather conditions such as fog, rain, and snow, all of which can impact signal quality and overall system reliability [14–19]. Despite these challenges, MWP techniques are increasingly important in modern radar systems, offering improved signal quality, greater flexibility in system design, and the potential for miniaturization and integration with other photonic systems.

For radar applications, MWP techniques offer reconfigurable high-bandwidth and high-frequency signal generation, down-conversion, up-conversion, de-chirping, and beam-forming functionalities [20–22].

Among these benefits, the high-bandwidth signal generation is particularly important, as range resolution is a measure of the ability to distinguish between objects and depends on the radar signal’s bandwidth, given by $\Delta R = \frac{c}{2B}$, where c is the speed of light and B is the bandwidth of the radar signal. The higher the bandwidth of the radar signal, the finer the range resolution, allowing for more precise measurements [23–26]. In contrast, achieving these capabilities with electronics is challenging, as electronics suffer from high path loss, electromagnetic interference, increased noise at higher frequencies and high-bandwidth signals, and limited reconfigurability for high-frequency operations.

10.2.1 MWP for Reconfigurable Radar Signal Generation

MWP offers the generation of arbitrary radar waveforms with reconfigurable parameters such as frequency, bandwidth, phase, and chirp rate. In the photonic domain, a radar signal with a bandwidth of 30 GHz has already been demonstrated [27]. High-bandwidth signals are crucial for achieving finer range resolution in object detection, especially when using LFM waveforms. The common techniques for photonics-based radar signal generation can be given as:

10.2.1.1 Photonic Heterodyning

This technique creates an optical signal with a beat frequency that can be transformed into a microwave signal by utilizing two lasers with different wavelengths or one mode-locked laser with multiple wavelengths. Various radar waveforms, including LFM, can be produced by varying and beating the wavelengths at the photodetector [28–31]. High stability, minimal phase noise, and a variety of waveforms can be achieved using the photonic heterodyning technique [32].

10.2.1.2 Photonic Multiplication

In such techniques, electro-optic modulator-generated sidebands are used to generate radar waveforms. Intended radar waveform with low frequency and bandwidth is fed to the modulator, which produces higher-order sidebands. Upon the beating of the higher-order sidebands in the photodetector, the microwave radar signal with high frequency and bandwidth can be generated. These techniques offer advantages such as high efficiency, low noise, and the ability to generate complex radar waveforms with high frequency and bandwidth multiplication. These techniques have the potential to enhance the performance and capabilities of radar systems for a variety of applications [33,34].

10.2.1.3 Optical Injection Technique

In this microwave radar signal generation technique, two laser beams beat in such a way that the injection of one beam causes a shift in the emission wavelength of the other lase. Upon injecting the external optical beam, also called master laser into a semiconductor laser, also called slave laser with varying intensity and wavelength, the redshift phenomenon occurs and the microwave signal can be generated upon beating the injected wavelength with the red-shifted wavelength of the semiconductor laser. This phenomenon can be used to generate radar waveforms with reconfigurable frequencies, bandwidths, and chirp rates [35–37].

10.3 Signal Processing in Photonic Radar System

In photonics-based radar (Ph-radar), the electronic signal processing tasks are similar to those in electronics-based radar. These tasks include designing radar waveforms tailored to specific applications, de-noising received echo signals, de-chirping, adaptive filtering, enhancing performance parameters such as range-Doppler and imaging resolution, and mitigating the effects of external radar interference. Discussing all of these signal processing challenges is out of the scope of this chapter. However, this chapter introduces radar-radar IM techniques for photonic radar systems, which are increasingly important due to the growing use of radars both for present time and in the future. These techniques are expected to advance radar technology and address emerging challenges.

10.3.1 Introduction to Radar-Radar Interference

Today, radars are widely used in a variety of fields, including remote sensing, defense, agriculture, industry, automotive applications, and consumer electronics. As radar applications increase, signals from external radar sources such as interference radar (IR) can disrupt legitimate radars, known as victim radars (VR). The serious issue arises when IR operates at the same or nearby frequency bands as the VR. This IR signal can severely affect the VR’s performance by adding noise and creating false targets. In a multi-radar environment, such interference can degrade the VR’s detection capabilities and pose risks to the safe and effective operation of radar systems. Therefore, mitigating interference in a multi-radar environment is essential.

Among the various radar waveforms, the LFM is the most prevalent. It can be described by:

$T_{x}\left( \left( ,t, \right) \right) = A_{tx}\cos\left( \left( ,\left( \left( ,{2\pi\left( \left( ,{f_{c} - \frac{B}{2}}, \right) \right)t + \pi\alpha_{tx}t^{2}}, \right) \right), \right) \right),\,\,\text{for}\,\, 0 \leq t \leq T$(10.1)

where Atx represents the amplitude of the transmitted signal, fc is the center frequency, B denotes the operating bandwidth, αtxis the chirp rate (B/T), and T is the time duration of the LFM signal. Over the period 0 ≤ t ≤ T, the frequency of the transmitted signal increases linearly from $f_{c} - \frac{B}{2}$ to $f_{c} + \frac{B}{2}$. When this transmitted signal reflects off K number of stationary targets, the received signal Rx((,t,)) can be expressed as

$R_{x}\left( \left( ,t, \right) \right) = \sum\limits_{k = 1}^{K}A_{Rk}\cos\left( \left( ,\left( \left( ,{2\pi\left( \left( ,{f_{c} - \frac{B}{2}}, \right) \right)\left( \left( ,{t - \tau_{dk}}, \right) \right) + \pi\alpha_{tx}\left( \left( ,{t - \tau_{dk}}, \right) \right)^{2}}, \right) \right), \right) \right),$(10.2)

where ARk is the amplitude of the reflected signal from the kth target, and τdkrepresents the time delay due to the distance between the radar and the kth target. The range resolution of the LFM signal, which indicates the ability to distinguish between two target objects, is proportional to the frequency shift and can be given as $\Delta R = \frac{c}{2B}$, where c is the speed of light and B is the bandwidth of the radar signal.

Now, consider the IR signal, which is also LFM and acts on the receiver. It can be expressed as,


$$R_{I}\left( \left( ,t, \right) \right) = \sum\limits_{y = 1}^{Y}A_{Iy}\cos\left( \left( ,\left( \left( ,{2\pi\left( \left( ,{f_{c} - \frac{B_{Iy}}{2}}, \right) \right)\left( \left( ,{t - \tau_{Iy}}, \right) \right) + \pi\alpha_{Iy}\left( \left( ,{t - \tau_{Iy}}, \right) \right)^{2}}, \right) \right), \right) \right)$$
(10.3)

where AIyis the amplitude of the yth IR signal, αIy is the chirp rate, and τIy is the delay of the IR signal relative to the VR signal. The chirp rate (αIy) of the IR signal is calculated as $\frac{B_{Iy}}{T_{Iy}}$, with BIy being the bandwidth and TIy the time duration of yth IR signal. When the chirp rates of the VR signal and IR signal are identical (αIy = αtx), this is termed coherent interference; if they differ (αIy ≠ αtx), it is called non-coherent interference. With the presence of IR signals, whether coherent or non-coherent, receiver’s output is a mix of the VR’s echo and the IR signal. For simplicity, consider a scenario with one VR and one IR source. The beating frequency caused by the IR signal RI(t), after de-chirping, is given by,

fbi = (αIy−αtx)t + αIyτIy(10.4)

Figure 10.1 depicts the LFM VR signal and its de-chirped signal at the receiver in the presence of an IR signal with either the same or different chirp rates. In Figure 10.1a-i, where the IR signal has the same chirp rate as the VR signal, two constant beat frequencies fbo and fbi are observed at the receiver’s output as shown in Figure 10.1a-ii. These beat frequencies fall within the bandwidth of the receiver and indicate the presence of the two target objects, including one ghost target as illustrated in the range profile of Figure 10.1a-iii. In Figure 10.1b-i, where the chirp rates are different, a constant beat frequency, fbo, from the actual target object is observed alongside a varying beat frequency fbi(t) as shown in Figure 10.1b-ii. This varying beat frequency introduces additional undesired frequency components in the received signal and increases the noise floor in the range profile as depicted in Figure 10.1b-iii. Both types of interference, whether coherent, Figure 10.1a or non-coherent, Figure 10.1b, significantly impair the VR’s detection performance in a multi-radar environment by creating ghost targets or increasing the noise floor. Figure 10.1 demonstrates a scenario with one target object and one interference signal acting in the received echo. But, in real-world applications, multiple object detection should be carried out along with the various interference signals. Various approaches can be implemented to minimize the effect of IR signals in a VR signal while operating in a multiple IR sources environment. This chapter introduces the techniques of mitigating IR effect through the design of interference-resistant radar waveform and through DL-based solutions.

10.3.2 Radar-Radar Interference Mitigation

Among various radar waveforms, the LFM waveform is popular for its superior pulse compression abilities, which enhance range-Doppler resolution. Nevertheless, the LFM waveform shows poor interference resistance, particularly when an IR signal interferes coherently with a VR signal. Most current IM approaches depend on sophisticated signal processing methods within the radar’s receiver. Consequently, designing radar waveforms with inherent IM capabilities is crucial for real-time objects detection. This chapter introduces the concept of interference-resistant waveform design. Additionally, recent advancement in DL-based IM techniques have gained attention to the radar research community, as these techniques can reconstruct the echo signal by suppressing the interference regardless of the types of VR and IR signal involved. Therefore, in this chapter, a DL-based IM technique for photonics-based radar systems is introduced.

10.3.2.1 Interference-Resistant Waveform Design

In a multi-radar environment, interference is a particularly significant factor contributing to performance degradation. As radars become more widely used across various applications, the operation of multiple radars with overlapping frequency ranges leads to interference. Interference increases the noise floor and introduces the ghost target objects into the range profile, which results in reduced detection capabilities and erroneous detections. To mitigate interference, various computationally complex signal processing techniques have been developed [31,39–44]. Though these methods can lead to latency and increased costs, interference-resistant transmission waveforms offer effective solutions to these problems. In electronics, examples of such solutions include random noise waveforms [45], phase-coded waveforms [46], multiple-chirped waveforms [47], and more. In the photonics domain, techniques like sparse-stepped frequency chirps [48] and chaotic radar [49] solutions have been proposed. Additionally, range-Doppler resolution is also a critical metric for detection accuracy. LFM waveform is popular due to its excellent compression characteristics which provide superior range-Doppler resolution. However, LFM waveform is less effective at resisting interference, particularly when the IR operates coherently to the VR. Thus, modified LFM waveforms are essential for achieving both excellent range-Doppler resolution and effective IM.

Parajuli et al. [50] introduced a modified LFM waveform known as photonically generated orthogonal up-down-chirp pair-based multi-chirp LFM (Ph-O-MLFM) waveform as an interference-resistant waveform while ensuring excellent resolution capability. This waveform is distinctive due to its orthogonal properties and the use of concatenated LFM waveforms with different chirp rates. The orthogonality of LFM signal enhances compression characteristics compared to using individual LFM signals [39,51]. This feature is incorporated into the Ph-O-MLFM waveform through the concatenation of up-down LFMs. Additionally, the use of concatenated LFM waveforms with varying chirp rates prevents coherent interference by shifting the signal-to-interference beating frequency outside the receiver’s bandwidth. As a result, the use of multiple concatenated LFMs effectively reduces the impact of interference in the range profile.

Figure 10.2 illustrates how IR LFM affects the beat frequencies (Δf) in the radar receiver (Rx) for two different radar waveforms: common photonics-based LFM (Ph-LFM) waveform and the Ph-O-MLFM waveform. Figure 10.2a-i and b-i show the transmitted, echo, and interference signals received for the Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM VR waveforms, respectively. As depicted in Figure 10.2a-ii, the Ph-LFM waveform experiences significant coherent interference because the signal-to-interference beat frequency falls within the radar Rx BW, making it difficult to suppress interference. Conversely, Figure 10.2b-ii demonstrates that for the multi-chirp Ph-O-MLFM waveform, most of the signal-to-interference beat frequency falls outside the Rx BW, thereby inherently diminishing the impact of the interference.

To demonstrate the detection capabilities of the Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM waveforms, the authors set up a simulated multi-radar environment where both waveforms were generated and utilized for object detection evaluation. For the Ph-LFM, a 2-GHz, 1-µs LFM signal was used, while the Ph-O-MLFM waveform was generated using two 2-GHz, 1-µs, three-chirp LFM signals fed into a dual-drive Mach-Zehnder modulator (DDMZM). By adjusting the DDMZM parameters, photonic-based bandwidth synthesis was achieved, resulting in 4-GHz, 1-µs, Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM radio frequency (RF) signals after photodetection. These waveforms are then combined with varying chirp rate 4-GHz LFM signals, and a detection analysis for two objects spaced 6 cm apart was conducted using a matched filtering technique.

Figure 10.3 illustrates the setup for generating and analyzing Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM RF signals in a multi-radar environment. This setup includes an MWP system based on DDMZM to produce RF radar signals through the photonic beating of the sidebands created by the DDMZM. The setup uses a CW laser operating at 0 dBm output power with a 10 MHz linewidth, and a photodetector with a thermal noise current spectral density of 10e−12 A/Hz1/2 to simulate realistic noise conditions. The RF signal from the photodetector is then amplified using an RF amplifier with a 20-dB gain and a noise current spectral density of 20e−12 A/Hz1/2 and filtered through a fourth-order Bessel bandpass filter.

In the multi-radar setup depicted in the dotted box in Figure 10.3, the VR unit, IR unit, and objects space unit are configured. The VR unit transmits the photonically generated RF Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM waveforms and receives echoes from two objects separated by ΔL = 6 cm. The IR unit generates 4-GHz LFM interference signals with varying chirp rates and periods, which are directed toward the VR unit’s receiver (Rx). These IR signals are mixed with the echoes, and additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) with an SNR of 0 dB simulates the reception of noisy echoes and interference signals. Object detection in the presence of LFM interference is assessed by analyzing the normalized cross-correlation (C-Corr.) amplitude, range resolution, and peak-to-sidelobe-level power (PSL) values in the obtained range profile.

To generate a 4-GHz Ph-LFM signal, a 2–4 GHz LFM driving signal with a 2-GHz bandwidth and a 1-µs time period is created and applied to both the RF1 and RF2 ports of the DDMZM with identical phases. The DDMZM operates using an optical-carrier suppressed double sideband (OCS-DSB) scheme, and the resulting optical spectrum is given in Figure 10.4a. Figure 10.4b depicts the amplitude-time diagram of the generated RF Ph-LFM signal, with the filtered RF spectrum as illustrated in Figure 10.4c. Consequently, a 2-GHz driving signal within the 2–4 GHz range is photonically synthesized by the DDMZM, generating a 4-GHz Ph-LFM signal with a frequency range of 4–8 GHz, as shown in the frequency-time diagram in Figure 10.4d.

To generate a 4-GHz Ph-O-MLFM signal, three up-chirp LFM signals with frequency ranges of 4–5.25, 5.25–5.5, and 5.5–6 GHz are concatenated and applied to the RF1 port of DDMZM. Similarly, the RF2 port of DDMZM is fed with three concatenated down-chirp LFM signals with frequency ranges of 8–6.75, 6.75–6.5, and 6.5–6 GHz. The up-chirp signals (4–6 GHz) and down-chirp signals (8–6 GHz) both have chirp rates of 3.75, 0.75, and 1.5 GHz/µs but with opposite signs. Each signal has a bandwidth of 2 GHz and a time period of 1 µs. In this case, the DDMZM is used in an optical double sideband (O-DSB) mode. By applying two 2 GHz driving signals to the RF1 and RF2 ports of the DDMZM, up-chirp and down-chirp signal pairs with opposite chirp rates are photonically combined, resulting in a 4-GHz, 1-µs Ph-O-MLFM RF signal after photodetection. The optical spectrum of the generated signal is shown in Figure 10.5a, and the amplitude-time diagram is presented in Figure 10.5b. Figure 10.5c depicts the filtered RF spectrum, while Figure 10.5d illustrates the frequency-time diagram of the Ph-O-MLFM signal. Using the photonics-based bandwidth synthesis with DDMZM, a 4-GHz RF output signal can be produced from a 2-GHz input RF signal in the case of Ph-LFM signal generation. Similarly, for Ph-O-MLFM signal generation, two 2-GHz input RF signals are used to generate a 4-GHz output RF signal.

To evaluate the performance of the generated signals, two object detection analyses were conducted. Figure 10.6a and b show the range profiles of objects detected without interference using the Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM signals, respectively. The −3 dB pulse widths of the main lobes are 3.8 cm for Ph-LFM and 4 cm for Ph-O-MLFM. The PSL is 12.8 dB for Ph-LFM and 11.6 dB for Ph-O-MLFM. Object detection accuracy is within a maximum error of 2 mm. Despite the inclusion of dissimilar chirp rate LFMs, which can enhance phase noise and degrade PSL and range resolution, the range resolution and PSL of the Ph-O-MLFM signal are comparable to those of the Ph-LFM signal. This performance is largely attributed to the orthogonality of the Ph-O-MLFM waveform, which reduces sidelobes’ amplitude due to its composition of orthogonal up-down LFM pairs.

To assess the effect of interference on the generated Ph-LFM and Ph-O-MLFM VR signals, LFM-IR signals with chirp rates and time periods of ILFM1(4 GHz/µs, 1 µs), ILFM2(8 GHz/µs, 0.5 µs), and ILFM3 (16 GHz/µs, 0.25 µs) were generated and combined with the VR signals. Figure 10.7 shows how these LFM interferences affect the range profile of Ph-LFM signal. Figure 10.7a-i, b-i, and c-i depict the frequency-time diagrams of the echo signals in the presence of ILFM1, ILFM2, and ILFM3 interference signals, respectively. The range profile in Figure 10.7a-ii shows that when Ph-LFM is subjected to coherent interference, a ghost target appears. However, with ILFM2 and ILFM3, the ghost target is not observed. Despite this, the PSL value is reduced by 0.5 dB compared to the scenario without interference.

A similar analysis is performed on the Ph-O-MLFM signal, as shown in Figure 10.8. In the range profile presented in Figure 10.8a-ii, the ghost target signal no longer appears, unlike in Figure 10.7a-ii. For all IR signals depicted in Figure 10.8a–c, a PSL of over 11 dB and a −3 dB pulse width of 4 cm are achieved. The objects remain resolvable without significant degradation compared to the scenario without interference, as illustrated in Figure 10.6b. Only a 0.4-dB decline in the PSL is observed, indicating the Ph-O-MLFM signal’s effectiveness in mitigating interference. The obtained range resolution of 4 cm is close to the theoretical range resolution of 3.87 cm for a 4-GHz LFM signal.

It was observed that in the obtained range profile, the Ph-LFM waveform suffers with coherent interference. However, the Ph-O-MLFM waveform effectively mitigates these interferences, achieving a PSL of over 11 dB and a range resolution of 4 cm, which is close to the theoretical range resolution value of the 4 GHz LFM signal. These results show the Ph-O-MLFM waveform’s suitability for use in multi-radar environments, offering excellent resolution and inherent resistance to interference.

10.3.2.2 Radar-Radar Interference Mitigation with Deep Learning

As in other fields, DL techniques have recently attracted considerable attention from the radar research community, particularly for IM. Among the various DL-IM techniques, convolutional neural network (CNN)-based methods [52–55] and recurrent neural network (RNN)-based methods [56–58] are commonly studied. In radar systems, IR signals distort the original echo signal’s time domain sequence by adding undesired interference components, which need to be suppressed. RNN-based techniques effectively address these sequence-based temporal regression problems and provide promising solutions. In this chapter, we introduce a long short-term memory (LSTM)-based multi-layer recurrent neural network (LSTM-RNN) to address interference issues in a multi-radar environment [59].

In Refs. [38,60], the authors introduced a robust LSTM-based RNN (LSTM-RNN) to suppress interference signals from the received echo. The setup shown in Figure 10.9 was created to demonstrate IM using LSTM-RNN. A VR LFM transmission signal with a bandwidth of 4 GHz (4–8 GHz) was generated. The VR signal was employed to detect two objects located 3 m away from the radar sensor, with a separation of 4 cm between each object.

Table 10.1 presents the parameters for VR and IR signals. The VR signal is generated with a 1-µs time period and a 4-GHz bandwidth. This VR signal is used to detect two objects, and the received echo serves as the output label for the LSTM-RNN. The external electronically generated LFM interference signals were created with uniformly distributed random chirp rates (1–20 GHz/µs), bandwidths (1–6 GHz), and AWGN noise equivalent to SNR of (0–20 dB) as shown in Table 10.1. A total of 70 interference signals with varying parameters were generated. To prepare the training and test datasets, the LFM interference signals were mixed with the echo signals. For testing purposes, the test signals with one to eight interference signals were selected. For testing purpose, different interference signals from those used in the training stage were chosen randomly to ensure that the trained model performs properly with various interference signals with different parameters.

Table 10.1 Dataset Preparation with VR and IR
IR and VR Signals Generation

Before applying the data signals to the LSTM-RNN, they were normalized with zero mean and unit variance normalization. Optimal hyperparameter values were determined through hyperparameter tuning using the grid search method. Obtained optimal hyperparameters are presented in Table 10.2. During the hyperparameters optimization process, the mean square error (MSE) and mean absolute error (MAE) values for each iteration were minimized using the Adam optimization algorithm [61].

Table 10.2 Optimum Hyperparameters
Hyperparameters Values
No. of layers 4
Nodes per layer (32,16,8,1)
Learning rate 0.004
Batch size 32
Epoch 25

Using four layers and a learning rate of 0.004, the optimal number of nodes per layer was determined to be (32, 16, 8, 1). The optimization function in the training process minimizes the loss function, which measures the error between the LSTM-RNN output (echo with interference suppression) and the expected output (echo without interference).

To evaluate the model’s performance, the R2 value is calculated on the test signals which can be given as

$R^{2} = \,\,\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,{1 - \,\,\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n = 1}^{N}\left\lbrack \left( ,{y\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,n, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack - \widetilde{y}\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,n, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack}, \right) \right\rbrack^{2}}, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack$(10.5)

where, N represents the sample length, y[n] denotes the expected value, and ỹ[n] denotes the calculated value by LSTM-RNN. In radar systems, external interference usually causes two main issues in the range profile: (i) an increase in the noise floor due to non-coherent interference, and (ii) the appearance of ghost targets due to coherent interference [62–64]. SINR and EVM values are used as quantitative measures of radar detection performance. Therefore, to assess the similarity in the range profiles between echo signals without interference and interference-suppressed echo signals, EVM and SINR calculations were conducted. The EVM value can be calculated as [54]

$\text{EVM} = \,\,\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n = 1}^{N}\frac{\left| \left| ,{S\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,n, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack - \widetilde{S}\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,n, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack}, \right| \right|}{\left| \left| ,{S\left\lbrack \left\lbrack ,n, \right\rbrack \right\rbrack}, \right| \right|}$(10.6)

where, |x| denotes the absolute value of the signal x, S̃[[,n,]] represents the normalized range profile value obtained for interference-suppressed echo signal, while S[n] denotes the value for the normalized range profile of the reference echo signal. Additionally, the SINR can be derived from EVM as: SINR  [[,dB,]] = 10 × Log  ((,1/EVM,)). The SINR calculation accounts not only for the remaining interference but also for all distortions present in the interference-suppressed echo signal relative to the reference echo signal [54].

Figure 10.10 shows the spectrograms at various stages. Figure 10.10a-i and b-i show the frequency-time diagrams of the transmitted and received echo signals, respectively. Figure 10.10b-i illustrates the frequency-time diagrams with multiple coherent and non-coherent interferences affecting the echo signal. Similarly, Figure 10.10c-i depicts the effect of multiple coherent interferences on the echo signal. Figure 10.10b-ii and c-ii show the recovered echo signals after applying the LSTM-RNN to the cases in Figure 10.10b-i and c-i, respectively. As shown in Figure 10.10b-ii and c-ii, the LSTM-RNN effectively suppresses multiple interferences.

Figure 10.11 presents the range profile results for different test scenarios. Coherent and non-coherent interference signals are added to the victim echo signals for testing purposes. Figure 10.11a corresponds to the case of both coherent and non-coherent interferences, as shown in Figure 10.10b, while Figure 10.11b corresponds to the case of coherent interferences acting upon the echo signal, as shown in Figure 10.10c. In both figures, the blue color represents the range profile results after mitigating interference with the LSTM-RNN, and the red color represents the range profile results with interferences. As observed, the interference significantly increases the noise floor. However, the LSTM-RNN effectively suppresses the interference-induced noise. The quantitative measure of effectiveness of the LSTM-RNN can be evaluated using R2, SINR, and EVM values.

Table 10.3 summarizes the average results (based on 15 trials) for test signals with 1, 4, and 8 interference signals. The same previously trained model was used to evaluate each test signal. During testing, the loss metrics (MSE/MAE) were calculated using the trained model. The results in Table 10.3 show that the designed model can recover the interference-suppressed echo signal from the received echo signal with multiple interference signals acting on the VR, achieving a mean R2 value greater than 92%. For the given signal scenario, the optimal hyperparameters resulted in an LSTM-RNN model with four layers, sufficient to achieve an R2 value of over 92% for all test data, demonstrating strong performance for regression problems. Thus, the obtained hyperparameters are optimal for the dataset used. Additionally, EVM values below 0.075 and SINR values above 11 dB were achieved for test signals with 1, 4, and 8 interferences, which are acceptable for practical scenarios [54].

Table 10.3 Model Evaluation
No. of Interferences R2 MSE MAE EVM SINR [dB]
1 92.8% 0.09 0.25 0.066 11.9
4 92.7% 0.091 0.26 0.068 11.81
8 92.4% 0.094 0.28 0.073 11.64
